Abstract (英文): Long-horizon reasoning remains a central challenge for large language models (LLMs) under sparse-reward regimes. We argue that this brittleness arises from two biases induced by complex reasoning spaces: an exploration bias, where models are drawn toward locally plausible but structurally unstable branches, and a compounding bias, where small local deviations accumulate across depth and suppress rare rewards. We introduce Symbolic Closure Analysis (SCA) as a theoretical lens characterizing how branching structures and sparse rewards induce these biases in long-horizon reasoning with local admissibility, and as a design principle for structural priors in less formal reasoning tasks. Motivated by this analysis, we propose SAGE (Structural Admissibility-Guided Exploration), a unified framework that injects structural guidance to alleviate exploration bias and compounding bias in long-horizon reasoning. SAGE combines two complementary structural guidance: algebraic sparsification, which projects locally admissible candidates onto operator-indexed algebraic subspaces to suppress spurious branching and mitigate exploration bias, and hyperbolic structural guidance, which embeds reasoning states into a negatively curved space to provide dense depth-wise signals and mitigate compounding bias. Across 12 benchmarks and 7 model families, SAGE outperforms competitive baselines. In particular, SAGE achieves up to an 8-fold improvement on the Andrews-Curtis problem, an open real-world long-horizon task. Code is available at: https://github.com/Susan571/SAGE-NeurIPS2026.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Scientific discovery begins where known problems end. There, AI systems must engage in exploration: framing hypotheses, designing experiments, and iterating on the results. However, evaluating this ability is difficult: (1) how to verify whether a genuinely new hypothesis holds, and (2) how to determine whether a system has discovered it through exploration or merely recalled related knowledge from pre-training data. To this end, we introduce ExplorationBench, which turns the wicked problem of evaluating scientific exploration into a concrete and tractable framework built on verifiable Alien Worlds: their rules are executable, so every answer can be checked exactly, and they conflict with familiar knowledge, so recall alone cannot solve the tasks. The benchmark contains two sandboxes, AlienCode (31 discovery targets, 70 tasks) and AlienLogic (24 discovery targets, 70 tasks). Each sandbox provides a flawed manual, task-specific environmental feedback, and a dedicated tool-call schema. Systems use these resources to explore the sandbox, then solve held-out tasks. We evaluate 10 AI systems and find that the strongest systems can acquire and apply unfamiliar rules, while performance varies substantially across trajectories and continued exploration can stall or reverse earlier gains. ExplorationBench represents a step towards AI systems that can acquire and apply genuinely new knowledge through exploration in unknown environments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large language model (LLM)-based clinical assistants are increasingly being integrated into electronic health record (EHR) systems, transforming how clinicians retrieve and synthesize information from patient records. Their safety and utility depend on rigorous evaluation, yet existing benchmarks are manually curated, costly to update, and rapidly become obsolete with evolving technological advancements. We present a scalable framework that automatically generates question--answer pairs from longitudinal EHR notes. Nineteen clinicians validate the benchmark generator, producing the Benchmark for Retrieving Information in EHRs (BRIE), a continuously maintainable evaluation dataset. Across nine LLMs and five inference strategies, state-of-the-art systems frequently omit clinically important information, particularly for questions requiring synthesis across multiple documents and encounters. Because the generator itself is validated, BRIE supports evaluations that static benchmarks cannot, including the generation of multiple answers that reflect variation in clinician reasoning for robust performance assessment and continuously refreshing benchmark content to guard against leakage. Our results demonstrate that scalable benchmark generation enables rigorous, up-to-date evaluation of clinical LLMs as they are deployed in rapidly evolving healthcare settings.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Latent world models are typically trained to predict factual transitions, whereas model predictive control (MPC) must compare alternative actions from the same state. A model can therefore achieve low factual prediction error yet poorly distinguish candidate actions. We introduce AD-WM, an action-discriminative joint-embedding world model for counterfactual MPC. AD-WM combines residual latent dynamics with predictor-level action-recovery regularization, using inverse dynamics and a normalized recovery objective motivated by conditional mutual information. Both objectives encourage planning transitions to preserve action information; their auxiliary heads are discarded at test time, leaving MPC unchanged. On OGBench-Cube, AD-WM improves hard-start success from 3.7% to 52.0% over a matched LeWM baseline and improves mean success over the reproduced baseline in four of five simulation environments. Planning diagnostics show that factual prediction error and whole-bank action ranking do not follow the closed-loop success ordering, whereas CEM-aligned elite regret tracks success more closely. With a frozen V-JEPA 2 encoder and matched DROID post-training, AD-WM also improves zero-shot transfer to our Franka setup, increasing basic pick-and-place success from 42.2% to 71.1% without lab-specific adaptation. These results suggest that world models for planning should preserve action-dependent differences needed for counterfactual selection, rather than optimize factual prediction accuracy alone. More videos and code are available at https://ad-wm.github.io/.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Leaf inclination angle (LIA) dynamics act as a rapid response mechanism to abiotic stress, regulating canopy energy balance and water use efficiency. While the adaptive value of diurnal LIA plasticity (e.g., paraheliotropism) is well-recognized in ecology, most current remote sensing algorithms and ecosystem models still treat canopy architecture as static and neglect stress-induced geometric adjustments. Furthermore, the diurnal dynamics of LIA under combined abiotic stresses, such as concurrent water deficit and salinity, still remain poorly understood. Recent advances in unmanned aerial vehicle (UAV) photogrammetry offer a promising approach for capturing LIA dynamics at high spatial and temporal resolution. However, accurately resolving fine scale, dynamic leaf movements in real environments using UAVs remains challenging. To address these gaps, we developed the Constraint-Assisted Point cloud fusion for Leaf scale Analysis (CAPLA), an integrated UAV analytical workflow that combines deep learning with Structure from Motion (SfM). CAPLA employs 2D semantic masks to strictly constrain 3D mesh reconstruction, effectively mitigating motion-induced artifacts. Independent validation against 19 plot level mean leaf angle (MLA) observations collected at 9:30 am and 12:00 pm yielded an R 2 of 0.89 and an RMSE of 0.9°, supporting plot level MLA estimation under the validated acquisition conditions. CAPLA was subsequently applied across five observation times to characterize diurnal canopy structural dynamics. Importantly, repeated measures analysis of the high frequency observations revealed significant effects of irrigation, salinity, and observation time on MLA, together with a significant irrigation × time interaction ( P = 0.0109), indicating that diurnal MLA trajectories differed among irrigation levels. In contrast, neither the irrigation × salinity interaction ( P = 0.8800) nor the irrigation × salinity × time interaction ( P = 0.9086) was significant. Descriptive differences in within-day variability were nevertheless observed among individual treatment combinations, highlighting the value of time-resolved structural monitoring for characterizing canopy responses to combined water and salinity stresses. These findings highlight the complex structural plasticity of canopies under interacting stresses, emphasizing the critical need to transition from static canopy assumptions to dynamic structural monitoring for improving ecosystem models and precision agriculture.

</details>

Abstract (英文): To improve understanding of air quality in Asia and support satellite validation, the Korean government launched the Pandora Asia Network (PAN) in 2021. The high temporal resolution and simultaneous retrievals of nitrogen dioxide (NO 2 ) and aerosol optical depth (AOD) from the PAN allow for detailed analysis of aerosol and precursor variability. This study evaluated NO 2 and AOD levels at various sites across major Asian cities with different air quality regulations, using consistent quality measurements from Pandora and satellite data. Generally, AOD from the Visible Infrared Imaging Radiometer Suite (VIIRS) Deep Blue product showed good agreement with the PAN retrievals, whereas TROPOspheric Monitoring Instrument (TROPOMI) generally underestimated NO 2 levels relative to Pandora, especially in highly polluted cities such as Dhaka and Singapore. The NO 2 -to-AOD ratio (NAR), which assesses aerosol characteristics associated with NO 2 sources, was higher in major cities such as Seoul and Singapore, whereas lower values were observed in areas with uncontrolled or limited combustion activity. Differences in NO 2 measurements between TROPOMI and Pandora led to discrepancies in NAR between satellite and ground-based data, particularly in Singapore, Islamabad, and Dhaka. Using high-frequency PAN sampling, we calculated the daily correlation coefficient between AOD and NO 2 (r d ) at each site. High values of r d were observed in Islamabad and Dhaka, where ammonia (NH 3 ) and NO 2 levels were elevated; both are important tracers of nitrate aerosol formation. This study estimated the NO x emission contribution factor to AOD (NCF) based on a statistical relationship among r d , NO 2 , NH 3 , and water vapor (H 2 O), which can serve as a diagnostic tool for evaluating aerosol emission sources across Asian countries.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Large-scale leaf area index (LAI) retrieval from satellite observations primarily relies on inverting canopy radiative transfer models (RTMs). Despite the widespread use of various inversion methods, how and why LAI retrievals vary with the method choice remains unclear. Here, within a unified Bayesian framework, we primarily characterized four representative methods as statistical point estimators: (i) numerical optimization (OPT) method; (ii) look-up table (LUT) method; (iii) LUT method with solution averaging (LUT-mean); and (iv) neural network (NN)-based method. Differences in their statistical principles lead to distinct bias-variance trade-offs, causing LAI retrieval RMSEs to vary by 30%–35% across simulated and in situ datasets. Our findings are: (1) The OPT and LUT methods approximate the maximum a posteriori estimator, exhibiting low bias but high variance and producing funnel-shaped scatter between retrieved and reference LAIs. (2) The LUT-mean and NN-based methods reduce variance by introducing bias, leading to overestimation in the mid-LAI range and underestimation in the high-LAI range, thereby producing S-shaped scatter. These characteristics enable the LUT-mean and NN-based methods to achieve lower RMSEs, with the NN-based method approximating the posterior mean estimator and attaining the lowest RMSE. Two alternative machine learning (ML)-based methods, i.e. , random forest (RF) and Gaussian process regression (GPR), share the same statistical interpretation as the NN-based method and exhibit similar retrieval performance. Our study identifies the bias-variance trade-off of inversion methods as one important yet previously underappreciated component of uncertainty in LAI monitoring and highlights the need to consider it in future LAI product development and evaluation.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Hydrothermal alteration is a key process in active volcanic systems, influencing edifice stability, fluid circulation, degassing pathways, and volcanic hazards. Remote sensing is widely used to map hydrothermal alteration; however, many approaches treat alteration-related surfaces as static targets, so their reliability, transferability, and process significance in active volcanic environments remain insufficiently evaluated. This study synthesizes commonly used remote sensing approaches and examines the behavior and interpretive implications of representative outputs at Lastarria volcano, which is an arid, high-altitude, vegetation-free system with active fumarolic fields. We compare visual interpretation, VNIR-SWIR reflectance products, pixel-based and sub-pixel mapping outputs, UAV thermal infrared observations, and LiDAR-derived surface-state information. The results show that visual interpretation, reflectance-based products, and pixel-based or sub-pixel outputs delineate broad alteration-related patterns, but their extent, internal structure, and interpretive meaning vary with methodological choices, surface mixing, and environmental surface states. Thermal infrared observations constrain apparent surface-temperature anomalies and fumarole-proximal activity, whereas LiDAR-derived products provide complementary information on surface state, morphology, and roughness. These observations do not converge on a single alteration representation; instead, they capture different aspects of the volcanic-hydrothermal system. Environmental, climatic, and surface controls, including exposure state, moisture, surface reworking, and seasonality, further limit direct transferability among volcanic settings. We suggest that remote sensing of hydrothermal alteration in active volcanic environments should move from deterministic mapping toward process-oriented and uncertainty-aware interpretation, with temporal persistence, spatial coherence, process plausibility, and independent support providing a basis for future multi-temporal analysis and monitoring.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Early warning of extreme river discharge events (ERDEs) is crucial for water resources management and disaster reduction in the Yangtze River Basin (YRB), yet substantial uncertainty remains in their prediction. This study proposes a novel strategy to enhance ERDE early warning by detecting its associated atmospheric circulation signals. We develop DetRF, a machine learning model that integrates anomaly detection with a balanced random forest ensemble. DetRF was trained and validated using ERA5 reanalysis data from 2000 to 2019 and independently applied to 2020–2024, with its early warning performance evaluated against satellite-based precipitation and reanalysis river discharge data. Across eight subregions of the YRB, DetRF effectively detects ERDE-related atmospheric circulation signals, achieving mean recall, precision, and F1 score values of 0.756, 0.506, and 0.576, respectively, and consistently outperforming benchmark machine learning models. The detected signals accurately capture key synoptic features associated with hydrological extremes, including the positions and intensities of troughs, vortices, and cyclones, as well as moisture transport. More than 99% of the detected signals are followed by precipitation within five days, while persistent precipitation occurs in over 67% of cases across both local and upstream regions. More importantly, an average of 93.8% of DetRF-detected circulation signals are followed by at least one ERDE within the subsequent five days. These findings establish a direct link between specific atmospheric circulation signals and subsequent hydrological extremes, offering a new pathway for extending ERDE early warning beyond conventional hydrological prediction. The proposed framework is particularly promising for regions where hydrological observations are sparse.

Abstract (英文): Leaf Area Index (LAI) is a fundamental variable for characterizing vegetation canopy structure and ecosystem functioning. However, persistent cloud contamination substantially constrains the spatiotemporal continuity of LAI products derived from polar-orbiting satellite observations. In contrast, geostationary satellites provide dense temporal sampling, offering a valuable observational basis for filling data gaps in polar-orbiting satellite products. To bridge this observational gap, this study develops an observation-complementary framework that integrates high-frequency Himawari-8 Advanced Himawari Imager (AHI) observations with MODIS LAI products to achieve LAI reconstruction in cloud-prone regions. Within this framework, AHI observations are first used to retrieve LAI at a spatial resolution of 2 km using a Random Forest model trained with a high-quality MODIS-based benchmark dataset, and are then downscaled to 500 m using MODIS-derived spatial texture information. The reconstructed AHI LAI is subsequently integrated into low-quality or missing MODIS pixels to generate a fused AHI-MODIS LAI dataset over China. Validation against in-situ measurements and cross-comparison results show that the fused product maintains reasonable numerical consistency and stable temporal trajectories. Data availability increases from 70.70% to 90.90% under the 4-day compositing cycle. These results indicate that geostationary-polar-orbiting observation complementarity provides a practical pathway for improving the continuity and usability of optical LAI products in persistently cloudy regions, while remaining subject to the constraints of optical observations and cross-scale reconstruction assumptions.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Afforestation changes the surface biogeochemical and biophysical processes and thus affects land surface temperature (LST). The historic studies on the LST effect of afforestation ignored the variation of energy exchange caused by the difference in forest structure such as LAI, vegetation fraction, and canopy height that is directly related to forest age. In this study, we constructed a model of the Land Surface Temperature Effect from Afforestation (LSTEA) considering forest age and afforestation intensity for different climate zones in China and then analyzed the biophysical effects on LST from historical and future afforestation in China. Results showed that (1) the LSTEA model revealed significant spatial and seasonal variability, with minimal warming effects in winter and pronounced cooling effects in other seasons. (2) from 1990 to 2020, the total national afforestation area reached 71.44 × 10 4 km 2 , of which 78.2 % originated from cropland and 12 % from grassland. The current afforestation has induced a grid-/national cooling effect of −0.35 ℃/-0.29 ℃ in summer and −0.26 ℃/-0.21 ℃ in winter by 2021. (3) The projected afforestation initiatives are projected to induce progressively intensifying cooling effects during 2030–2100. By 2100, it will bring a summer grid-/national cooling effect of −1.29 ℃/-0.51 ℃, −0.90 ℃/-0.35 ℃ and −0.75 ℃/-0.28 ℃ under the SSP-RCP2.6, SSP2-RCP4.5 and SSP5-RCP8.5 scenario respectively. The projected years of 2060 in SSP1-RCP2.6, 2070 in SSP-RCP4.5, and SSP-RCP8.5 will be the time that the maximum of LST effects in the near decades. Our study highlights the important role of afforestation as a mitigation strategy for climate change.

</details>

Abstract (英文): High spatiotemporal resolution land surface temperature (LST) reconstruction is essential for analyzing land-atmosphere interactions and thermal environmental dynamics. Thermal infrared observations, the primary source of regional and global LST, are affected by cloud contamination, scale discrepancies, and distribution shifts. Meanwhile, reconstruction methods struggle to provide both hourly continuity and kilometer-scale detail, and cross-scale fusion often causes spatial over-smoothing. To address these limitations, we propose ST-LSTrans, a two-stage Transformer framework for reconstructing regional hourly 1 km all-weather LST. It uses cross-scale transfer learning with global pre-training on hourly ∼6.25 km CLDAS LST to capture regional thermodynamic evolution and local fine-tuning on four-times-daily 1000 m MODIS LST to recover kilometer-scale thermal heterogeneity. A KL divergence constraint is introduced to reduce distribution shifts and spatial over-smoothing during cross-scale fusion. Validation results based on multi-source datasets show that the global pre-training stage achieved an average RMSE of 1.13 K, while the local fine-tuning stage achieved an average RMSE of 1.06 K, with the minimum RMSE reaching 0.87 K. Independent Landsat validation showed that the reconstructed product achieved a scene-averaged RMSE of 2.98 K and improved upon the original CLDAS background field. Additional evaluations against VIIRS VNP21, which is generated using an independent temperature-emissivity separation algorithm, confirmed the cross-sensor consistency of the reconstructed LST. Ground-based observations further demonstrated that ST-LSTrans preserved realistic diurnal temperature cycle characteristics under both clear-sky and persistent cloudy conditions. SHAP analysis further revealed scale-dependent transitions in LST driving mechanisms from coarse-scale atmospheric forcing to kilometer-scale terrain and land surface controls. This study provides a unified and interpretable modelling framework for high spatiotemporal resolution LST reconstruction and driving mechanism analysis.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Remote sensing image super-resolution is an important approach for enhancing the spatial detail of Earth observation data. In super-resolving Sentinel-2 multispectral imagery from 10 m to 2.5 m, conventional single-image super-resolution (SISR) methods are largely constrained by purely pixel-driven mechanisms. As a result, they often produce structural discontinuities, blurred edges, and chromatic artifacts when reconstructing complex human-made objects such as narrow roads and farmland boundaries during cross-scale detail reconstruction. To address these limitations, this study proposes a geo-semantic-guided remote sensing super-resolution framework, termed AdaGeo-SR. The framework incorporates dense spatial feature maps extracted from AlphaEarth Foundation (AEF) as geo-semantic priors, providing additional geo-semantic and topological cues for the reconstruction process. To better leverage high-level semantic features, we further design a degradation-simulation-based feature balancing mechanism. By introducing specific degradation conditions during training, this mechanism encourages the network to reduce its over-reliance on the original pixel cues, thereby enhancing its use of external geo-semantic priors. Experiments on the OpenSR-Test and WorldStrat benchmarks show that AdaGeo-SR effectively suppresses structural distortions and hallucination artifacts while maintaining reasonable spectral consistency, leading to improved local geometric coherence. In addition, downstream road extraction experiments using SAM_Road on the NAIP test set further demonstrate that the super-resolved images produced by the proposed framework help reduce mask fragmentation and topological discontinuities. These results highlight the potential of the proposed framework for downstream structure-sensitive tasks and support the generation of structurally more reliable 2.5 m imagery for practical remote sensing applications.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Observations from the Gravity Recovery and Climate Experiment (GRACE) and GRACE Follow-On (GRACE-FO) provide essential information on terrestrial water storage anomalies (TWSA), from which groundwater storage anomalies (GWSA) can be derived. However, the approximately 11-month mission gap and the coarse spatial resolution of the original products constrain their application in regional groundwater monitoring and water-resource assessment. This study developed an integrated spatiotemporal reconstruction framework for the Three-North Region of China by combining historical phase-constrained gap filling with physics-guided global–local downscaling. First, a Kling-Gupta Efficiency-based singular spectrum analysis method (KGC-SSA) was developed to reconstruct the mission gap without external forcing data. Second, a physics-guided global–local-residual geographically weighted extreme gradient boosting framework (GLR-GWXGB) was developed to downscale GWSA to 1 km under grid-wise coarse-scale consistency constraints. KGC-SSA yielded a mean ensemble reconstruction spread of 0.06 cm during the mission gap and performed broadly comparably to benchmark methods. Relative to the global benchmark model, GLR-GWXGB improved internal validation performance, preserved regional-scale consistency with the original GRACE-derived GWSA, and enhanced local spatial detail. Correlations with groundwater observations improved at more than 70% of monitoring sites. The reconstructed 1 km product further captured persistent groundwater-storage decline during 2002–2022, with intensified depletion after approximately 2012, while retaining the dominant long-term and seasonal characteristics of the original series. These results demonstrate that the proposed framework provides a robust approach for generating temporally continuous and spatially refined groundwater-storage information from GRACE/GRACE-FO observations.

</details>

Abstract (英文): This study developed a coal mine methane remote-sensing monitoring framework centered on adaptive background suppression using high-spatial-resolution multispectral data from Sentinel-2. The framework integrates methane column enhancement retrieval, automated plume detection, and emission flux estimation. Specifically, bilateral filtering and the multi-band single-pass (MBSP) method were used to retrieve methane column enhancements; a methane plume detection algorithm combining NDVI-based stratification, adaptive segmentation, morphological processing, and wind-field constraints was developed; and coal mine methane emission fluxes were quantified using the integrated mass enhancement (IME) method. Application to a pilot coal enterprise in Changzhi during 2024 demonstrated that the proposed framework effectively reduced weak vegetation-background effects and heterogeneous high-intensity interference while preserving plume topological consistency. Sentinel-2 also provided an observation frequency more than eight times higher than that of EMIT. The mean satellite-derived emission rate for the pilot enterprise was 1023.57 ± 266.84 kg ⋅ h − 1 , with a mean relative deviation of 14.47% from ground-based measurements. The relatively large deviations and daily-scale fluctuations observed during some periods were mainly attributed to uncertainties associated with the spectral resolution of Sentinel-2, surface heterogeneity, algorithm parameter settings, and ERA5 wind-field data. A comparison with the EDGAR inventory revealed broad consistency in regional spatial emission patterns. However, the higher-resolution Sentinel-2 data detected potential coal-related emission signals in several grid cells absent from EDGAR, accounting for 22.2% of the potential emission grids identified in Changzhi. On a regional scale, EDGAR’s annual emission estimate for Changzhi was 32.11% higher than the Sentinel-2 annualized estimate derived in this study.

Abstract (英文): Railway-corridor deformation monitoring requires timely identification of localized displacement anomalies and characterization of their temporal evolution. Time-series Interferometric Synthetic Aperture Radar (InSAR) provides spatially dense and high-precision displacement observations from repeated SAR acquisitions, but InSAR-based railway studies have largely remained rate-centered, leaving the kinematic information embedded in displacement time series insufficiently exploited. This study develops a kinematic interpretation framework that integrates InSAR displacement time series, transfer learning-based Temporal Convolutional Network (TCN) classification, and multi-source environmental data to characterize railway-corridor deformation in terms of its occurrence, temporal evolution, and environmental context. Sentinel-1 images acquired from January 2022 to December 2024 were processed to retrieve LOS displacement rates and displacement time series along the Kunming–Yuxi section of the China–Laos Railway. To reduce dependence on labeled InSAR samples, a transfer learning strategy was adopted, in which the TCN was pre-trained using simulated displacement sequences and fine-tuned with labeled InSAR observations to classify six kinematic patterns: stable, linear, step, piecewise linear, power-law, and undefined. The corridor is generally stable, with a mean displacement rate of −0.26 mm/year and stable points accounting for 77.96% of observations. Linear and step patterns account for 12.66% and 7.87%, respectively. Strong subsidence zones, where the localized displacement rate reaches −28.43 mm/year, are dominated by linear deformation. Pattern-based interpretation further shows that non-stable deformation is associated with Quaternary unconsolidated sediments, paddy soils, fine-grained soil textures, and urban expansion areas. These results indicate that InSAR-derived kinematic patterns provide an effective interpretive layer for linking railway-corridor displacement, temporal evolution, and environmental context.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The precise characterization of dynamic Earth surface processes relies on time-series remote sensing imagery with both high spatial fidelity and frequent temporal coverage. While spatiotemporal fusion (STF) bridges this resolution trade-off by integrating complementary observations, its practical application remains constrained by the idealized ”clear-sky assumption”. This requirement often favors the selection of temporally distant cloud-free acquisitions over temporally closer but cloud-contaminated observations, potentially reducing the relevance of reference information. To address this limitation, we propose CloudSTF, a robust framework designed to achieve high-fidelity reconstruction by effectively exploiting partially occluded observations. Our approach employs a Mask-guided Multi-scale Swin Transformer (M 2 ST) encoder to capture cloud spatial patterns and suppress noise propagation during feature extraction. This is coupled with a Cross-temporal Memory-guided Fusion (CTMF) module that adaptively integrates temporal trends with textural details retrieved from a spatio-temporal memory bank. To validate this paradigm, we introduce the Global Cloud-shrouded Regions (GCR-STF) benchmark, a geographically distributed dataset comprising 24 representative agricultural and urban sites across six continents. Extensive experiments demonstrate that CloudSTF consistently outperforms state-of-the-art methods. Additional assessments across varying cloud densities and diverse landscapes further demonstrate the robustness and applicability of CloudSTF under realistic cloud-contaminated conditions. These results suggest that CloudSTF provides a reliable solution for continuous Earth monitoring in realistic, cloud-prone environments. Source code and the GCR-STF benchmark are available at https://github.com/Sichen-Lu/CloudSTF .

</details>

Abstract (英文): Supraglacial lakes are widely distributed across the Greenland Ice Sheet (GrIS) each summer. The resulting meltwater-enhanced ablation effect plays a critical role in understanding the GrIS surface mass balance but remains poorly quantified. Inspired by the insight that longer meltwater persistence leads to stronger meltwater-enhanced ablation, this study proposes a novel method to directly estimate supraglacial lake bottom ablation rates at centimeter-per-day scale using satellite observations. First, we map multi-temporal supraglacial lake extents from Sentinel-2 imagery to estimate cumulative meltwater-covered days. Then, we derive ablation depths at the lake bottom and surrounding bare ice by differencing ICESat-2 repeat track data acquired before and during the melt season. Next, we perform linear fitting between lake bottom ablation depths and cumulative meltwater-covered days along the ICESat-2 tracks to calculate both the lake bottom ablation rate and the bare ice ablation rate. Finally, we evaluate the magnitude of the meltwater-enhanced ablation effect. We apply this method to 26 supraglacial lakes across the GrIS and identify a positive linear relationship between lake bottom ablation depths and cumulative water-covered days (mean Pearson correlation coefficient r = 0.70 ± 0.14, p < 0.001). The fitted mean lake bottom ablation rate (4.50 ± 1.15 cm/day) significantly exceeds the mean bare ice ablation rate (1.98 ± 0.52 cm/day). Meltwater in supraglacial lakes therefore enhances ablation rate by ∼ 1.3 ± 0.5 times, with values ranging from 0.6 to 2.4, consistent with previous field measurements (1.1–1.4 times) and numerical model simulations (1.1–1.7 times). In summary, this study provides a first quantitative satellite-based estimate of meltwater-enhanced ablation across the GrIS under varying ablation intensities, and may improve our understanding of GrIS surface melt process.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The pronounced thermal radiation anisotropy observed in rapidly urbanizing areas arises from the complex interplay of solar downward shortwave radiation (DSWR), building geometry, and local topography. To address this, we propose a novel framework, termed GRay-AU3SM-IRT, for the analysis of urban thermal anisotropy (UTA). The study was conducted in a representative mountainous city in Chongqing, China. A specific 0.4 × 0.4 km 2 area, characterized by buildings and terrain on 10° slopes, was selected for analysis at hourly resolution. The simulations showed strong agreement with measured data, achieving a root mean square error below 0.46 K and a coefficient of determination exceeding 0.79. The analysis reveals that UTA shows pronounced diurnal variations: it intensifies gradually in the morning, peaks at solar noon with clear hotspot formation, and generates secondary hotspots in the afternoon due to changing solar azimuth and slope effects. The morning striped distribution pattern caused by building geometry diminishes with declining solar irradiance and disappears by afternoon. Diurnal trends in the UTA Effect (UTAE), directional brightness temperature (DBT), DBT Effect (DBTE), and DSWR are generally aligned. Notably, UTAE and DBTE peak before DSWR, while both maximum and minimum DBT peak later. The proposed framework offers key advantages: precise multi-lap UAV navigation, minimized motion artifacts, consistent temporal sampling via adaptive velocity control, gap-filling through simulation, and spatio-temporal outlier correction. It provides critical data for angle normalization and correction across diverse urban environments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Chlorophyll a (Chl-a) is widely used to indicate eutrophication and bloom risk in inland waters, but daily Sentinel-3 observations alone cannot resolve transient sub-daily variability represented in partially observed 4-hourly in situ Chl-a records. This study therefore develops VGTA (Vision Mamba–GKOA–TCN-Attention), a staged remote-sensing-assisted and in situ-constrained framework that integrates daily Sentinel-3 OLCI-derived priors with partially observed 4-hourly in situ Chl-a sequences and binary observation masks. A 20-dimensional OLCI-derived feature space provides spectral–spatial priors for Chl-a estimation and bloom-risk state identification. GKOA optimizes TCN-Attention to reconstruct masked values within partially observed 4-hourly in situ Chl-a sequences. Vision Mamba outperformed CNN– and Transformer-based models in Chl-a estimation, achieving 94.8% accuracy, a Macro-F1 score of 0.909, and a Bloom-F1 score of 0.870. Ablation experiments confirmed the contributions of bidirectional contextual modeling, positional embedding, selective state-space modeling, and gating. Under Missing Completely at Random, block-missing, and real missing scenarios, GTA improved reconstruction performance by 12.71–55.54% relative to the best baseline models. Uncertainty increased once continuous gaps reached seven days, defining a practical boundary of data-driven reconstruction. Chl-a fluctuations and bloom-risk transitions can unfold within a day, and daily satellite snapshots may blur their timing or miss short events. Reconstruction of masked time steps within partially observed 4-hourly in situ Chl-a sequences can support earlier identification of bloom-risk transitions, more timely field sampling and management responses, and temporally finer monitoring of inland waters.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Predicting tree mortality hotspots driven by drought remains challenging, since vulnerability to drought is strongly influenced by climate, landform, and substrate. We propose a novel reference-anchored, quantile-envelope susceptibility model-implemented through a reproducible workflow- that exports mortality-resemblance (high-risk) environments from three confirmed tree-mortality footprints (training AOIs), based on semi-arid conifer and evergreen sclerophyllous forests in Cyprus, to produce wide screening layers. The approach combines (i) physiographic similarity derived from AOI-calibrated envelopes for slope, multi-scale topographic position and curvature (broad quantiles; majority-rule), (ii) exposure constraints via circular aspect windows, warm-season solar loading (April-September) and substrate (lithology) filtering, (iii) a drought severity gate (SPEI-12 (Standardized Precipitation-Evapotranspiration Index-12) ≤ -1.4), and (iv) Landsat event-year spectral collapse relative to a fixed 3-year pre-event baseline, coupled with a year + 1 persistence rule to suppress transient stress. Our within-AOI evaluation (forest-only) showed substantial representativeness (coverage 70.49–85.35%), while recovering most reference mortality pixels (recall 67.62–100%). In 2025, two independent mortality sites were identified in Cyprus, coinciding with exceptional drought (SPEI < -2) and a sharp decline in the Normalized Burn Ratio (NBR) consistent with foliage browning, and occurring within high-risk zones as mapped in the final risk map. As a plausibility check, both 2025 sites fell within pre-mapped high-risk zones, with 83.50% and 82.69% of their forested area exceeding HEAT ≥ 0.60 and mean risk scores of 84.91% and 75.55%, respectively. With 18.88% of Cyprus’s total forest flagged as high-risk, the probability of this co-occurrence being random is p ≈ 0.036 (binomial test). Anchoring calibration to local mortality footprints makes the approach readily transferable to other regions with documented events.

</details>

Abstract (英文): The rapid expansion of clean energy systems, particularly wind and solar power, has made large-scale monitoring of related infrastructure using high-resolution multispectral satellite imagery increasingly important yet challenging. Conventional wind turbine detection approaches rely on pixel-level analysis of high-resolution imagery or sliding-window strategies. These methods often suffer from computational redundancy, low efficiency, and false-positive accumulation, making them inadequate for large-area surveys and operational monitoring. To address these limitations, this study proposes a Targeted–Refined Framework that integrates AlphaEarth Foundations (AEF) with high-resolution Jilin-1 imagery. In the Targeted Spatial-Prior Generation Stage, the Unified Spatial Prior Generation Framework (USPGF) is developed to generate high-recall wind turbine candidate regions from AEF land-surface semantic embeddings, thereby replacing exhaustive full-area search with targeted candidate screening. In the Refined Detection Stage, the candidate regions are spatially matched to the corresponding high-resolution Jilin-1 image tiles, which are then processed using a YOLO-based model for refined wind turbine localization. Experimental results showed that fewer than 5 % of the high-resolution image tiles were retained by the Targeted Spatial-Prior Generation Stage, and the proposed framework achieved a recall of 96.85 %, a precision of 98.41 %, and an F1-score of 97.63 % on the Tianjin test set. Furthermore, the proposed Targeted–Refined Framework was directly applied to the Beijing–Hebei (BH) region for cross-regional evaluation. In this evaluation, the framework produced 1,580 detections, including 1,512 true positives and 68 false positives, and achieved an F1-score of 91.39 %, indicating favorable cross-regional generalization performance. Overall, this framework provides an efficient and scalable solution for large-scale detection of spatially sparse objects and offers a practical approach for infrastructure inventory and monitoring of wind farms and similar assets.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Early-season forest decline in temperate broadleaved stands often lacks detectable biochemical stress signals, manifesting primarily through reduced foliage density and increased visibility of non-photosynthetic vegetation (NPV). This study investigated the spectral separability of green leaves and NPV components under varying levels of canopy defoliation. Using a crane platform, we collected in situ leaf- and canopy-level spectrometer measurements alongside co-aligned photography to quantify the visible fraction of NPV. We analyzed the multi-angular reflectance characteristics and evaluated 35 vegetation indices (VIs) —including green biomass metrics and specialized NPV indices —using Random Forest to identify optimal spectral and geometric configurations. Sunlit young foliage exhibited high forward scatter, creating a nadir reflectance minimum. At canopy level, a nadir minimum was driven by the gap distribution resulting from sustainable forest management practices. Consequently, the optimal viewing angle for top-ranking VIs (e.g., SNC BAO , Ctr 2 , mNDVI 705 ) was near the hotspot geometry, minimizing shadowing and forest floor interference. While the total visible fraction of NPV remained mostly insensitive to viewing angles, its shaded proportion varied. VIs targeting green vegetation via red-edge and NIR domains consistently outperformed dedicated NPV indices based on SWIR cellulose and lignin absorption. This is attributed to low upper-canopy SWIR transmittance, which obscured the signals of the largely shaded NPV fraction. Top-performing VIs achieved R 2 = 0.92 for NPV visibility prediction, improving by 21 % over the best SWIR indices and 11 % over the nadir view. To accurately detect initial defoliation and overcome forest floor interference, adopting off-nadir viewing geometries is advantageous for capturing the optimal signal.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Turbidity is a key indicator of water quality and particulate dynamics, making its accurate monitoring essential for aquatic ecosystem management. While satellite remote sensing enables large-scale turbidity observation, products derived from different sensors often exhibit inconsistencies due to differences in spectral responses, residual atmospheric effects, and viewing geometries. Effectively integrating such multi-source data in the absence of ground truth remains a significant challenge. Here, we propose Gaussian mixture model-based soft-min (GMMS), a fully unsupervised fusion framework in which neither model construction nor weight estimation relies on ground-truth turbidity observations. GMMS integrates Gaussian mixture modeling with a soft-min weighting strategy to identify latent error-structure contexts and adaptively assign sensor weights according to relative residual stability. By exploiting inter-sensor error characteristics, the framework enables context-aware data fusion. At the overall scale, GMMS significantly outperforms triple collocation (TC) and extended triple collocation for maximized correlation (ETCC) ( p < 0.05). Compared with in situ turbidity observations, GMMS increases the correlation coefficient from 0.798 to 0.841 and reduces the RMSE from 16.849 to 15.921 TU. Site-level evaluations further indicate enhanced robustness and reduced performance variability across monitoring stations. Simulation experiments confirm that GMMS achieves the greatest and most stable performance gains under conditions of moderate-to-high error-structure separability. Overall, GMMS provides an interpretable and transferable unsupervised fusion framework for improving multi-source satellite-derived turbidity estimates in complex riverine environments.

</details>

Abstract (英文): Wildfires exert profound impacts on global ecosystems; however, accurate satellite-based burn area extraction is frequently confounded by the inherent mixed-pixel issue and complex atmospheric conditions. Although sub-pixel mapping (SPM) can resolve the mixed pixel issue, the extent to which its extraction accuracy depends on initial atmospheric preprocessing under diverse haze and smoke conditions remains poorly quantified. Furthermore, the conditions under which such preprocessing remains effective, and the boundary of its operational envelope, are not well understood. This study introduces a Smoke-Robust Sub-pixel Mapping (SRSPM) protocol to systematically diagnose atmospheric preprocessing efficacy prior to SPM. Using both simulated and real atmospheric states across five distinct fire regions, we systematically evaluate the sensitivity of sub-pixel fire perimeter delineation to different levels of haze contamination. Our findings demonstrate that atmospheric scattering inherently degrades the spatial allocation capabilities of SPM, leading to severe omission errors. Restoring contaminated spectral signals is shown to be essential, rather than merely beneficial, for accurate mapping when haze contamination is substantial. Crucially, our quantitative diagnosis reveals the operational limits of the SRSPM protocol: the dehazing benefit grows with haze intensity and remains substantial up to the strongest simulated contamination ( w = 1); in one region it turns positive only beyond w ≈0.73, delineating the quantitative envelope of the SRSPM protocol. Overall, this work establishes the SRSPM protocol as a practical diagnostic tool for quantifying the interaction between atmospheric correction and sub-pixel spatial allocation, providing empirical guidance for operational disaster monitoring in complex atmospheric environments.

</details>
